best ways to expand your vocabulary quickly first up learn word families a
word family is basically a group of words that come from the same root or
base called the word stem this is one of the most effective strategies for
building your vocabulary fast so instead of memorizing a single word you study a
collection of related words this approach helps you see patterns
understand word formation and learn how different forms are used in context all
of which make your language learning more practical and efficient take this
example Calen which means to buy this is the base verb now if we add a prefix
like Fair it becomes fa Cen and means to sell
Oren which means to shop so instead of fair we would add in and it becomes to
do grocery shopping in Calen or de C the purchase this noun is
formed from the verb Cen then we also got this C house literally the buying
house so in English we would say the department store
or the purchase agreement the seller or the salesman or
saleswoman or just de the sale notice how the word stem Cal evolves into
different words just by adding prefixes suffixes or turning it into a noun in
this example you didn't just learn what Calin means you also picked up seven
other related words from the same word family so learning related words like
this is not only expanding your vocabulary quickly but also helps you
understand spelling patterns and grammar Concepts more easily another fun
vocabulary activity is the poit challenge if you have been around for a
little bit you've probably already heard me talk about the Post-it challenge but
I'll keep mentioning it because this one is not only fun but also highly
effective simply write down new words on a posted note and stick them around your
house or workspace this constant exposure to words throughout your day
helps reinforce the meaning and usage you'll be amazed at how much more you
remember just by having them Insight regularly if you want to take it one
step further you can also write an example sentence with the activity
attached to that item the next tip I have for you is
learning one word in multiple contexts or word combination a great way to
deepen your understanding of German is to learn one word and explore how it's
used in different contexts and combinations this strategy helps you see
the versatility of a word and builds a more natural practical vocabulary take
the verb this means to make or do for example this word can be used in various
common phrases to throw a
party to take a break to go for a walk or to take a
walk to make music to be

next tip for you guys is to learn homonyms so homonyms are words that
sound or are spelled the same but have different meanings in German many words
have the same form which can be confusing especially in speaking they
are a powerful way to expand your vocabulary because you can learn
multiple meanings of one single word and use it in different contexts here are
some common German homonyms and also example sentences for example debank
debank can mean the bench as in of bank I am sitting on the bench but
the bank can also mean the bank bank I'm going to the bank or this
example does schlo you might have heard of the famous castle schlo
noin so yes Das means the castle Das is gross the castle is big
but Theos can also mean the lock dasos is caput the lock is broken but this
concept or strategy doesn't only apply for nouns you can also use it for verbs
like this verb for example to pull this means to be
physically pulling something like for example
is I pull the door open you'll also see the word on Doors in public areas very
often as well but Cen can also mean to move so for example
Vin we are moving to Berlin or the verb G which means to go like for
example I go home but can also mean to work or to function so for example if
you say D or D N meaning that works or that doesn't work and of course this
concept also applies for adjectives like for example the adjective BL which is
the color blue is BL the sky is blue but in
coloquial German blow can also mean drunk like for example if you say is
total he is completely drunk or another adjective l l could mean light as in p
is light the package is light but light can also mean easy the task was easy or the opposite
of that could mean heavy or you might have guessed it it can also mean
difficult so learning homonyms is super helpful because it lets you pick up more
meanings of just one word and make your vocabulary grow faster next I've got a
classic for you reading it's no surprise that reading is one of the best ways to
build your vocabulary whether it's books articles blogs or even social media

make it a habit to read something every day even if it's just for a few minutes
so here's a great tip to make the most out of your reading highlight new words
but don't go overboard focus on like 10 to 15 new words for reading session if
you find yourself highlighting a lot of words you don't know it might be an

description my next tip is to keep a vocabulary diary so keeping a vocabulary
diary is a simple but highly effective strategy for learning German the idea is
to write down 5 to 10 new words that you come across during your day making them
meaningful and highly relevant to your real life experiences this approach
helps you learn words in context which improves your ability to remember and
use them in future conversations so for example while sitting at the bus stop
you might notice a bird if you don't know what the German word for bird is
which is the F take a moment to look it up and write it down the next time you

they engineer or the engineering and by adding these words to your diary you're
learning not just vocabulary but also how to use them in different contexts so
by doing this exercise ideally at the end of the day you have five to 10 new
words that are highly related to what you experienced during the day and this
technique reinforces your memory and helps words stick longterm because
you're associating them with your personal experiences so here you have it

I went from zero to fluent in German and
you can copy exactly what I did. No fluff, no secret methods, just a system
that works. Here's a quick question for you. How many words do you think you
need to know to understand the majority of spoken German? The answer is around
3,000 words. Just 3,000 words can unlock 95% of spoken German for you. But if you
have ever tried learning a foreign language, you probably know that simply
learning words is not enough. You need to hear them in context over and over
again to really understand and start using them. And that brings me to the
first and most essential part of learning German that I want to share
with you. Comprehensible input. Comprehensible
input is about consuming content that you can mostly understand, even if there
are some words or grammar that you don't know yet. So it should be challenging
enough to push you forward but not so hard that you feel lost. Let me give you
a personal example here. When I had approximately B2 in German, I thought I
was advanced enough to start reading some serious stuff. So I decided to
combine two of my interests, philosophy and learning German. I chose the
original German book for it, which was Nisha's Pragustra. But of course, it
didn't go as planned and I gave up after just a couple of pages.
I mean, what else would you expect from trying to read sentences that consist of
60 words like this one? And it's not just about the length of the sentences,
but also the style of the language that Nit is using here. So, instead of
Nichze, I picked up something that was more appropriate for my level, which was
Shaka by Stefan. That was comprehensible input for me. This book was still
challenging. It was a bit above my level, but I could still guess the
meaning of some words from the context. And that's the key. This method applies
to any kind of input, whether it's books, podcasts, or movies. They have to
be level appropriate for you. And you might be wondering where can you
actually find this kind of books or movies. When it comes to books, there is
a big variety of literature that still tells the original story, but the
vocabulary was adapted to different levels. So you can search for this
adapted books online like for example Da Vanlung by France Kafka. As for movies
or series here are some of my favorite ones. For A1 learners I recommend Nikas.
For A2 learners check out Extra of Deutsch or Nikasv for A2. And for B1
learners I recommend Turkish FFinga. For B2 and C1 learners you can already start
watching regular German YouTube channels and documentaries like SWR do. And if
you want to find more amazing and free resources for learning German, I have an
entire video about it which will be linked in the description below. Once
you have started to learn German with comprehensible input, the next step is
to start using it even more actively with this powerful method, retelling
stories. This one is probably the most useful thing I did when I was learning
foreign languages. Now, this method does take some discipline, and it's nice to
have someone to practice it with, like a teacher or a friend. The retelling
method perfectly combines with comprehensible input that we talked
about earlier. And this is how it works. You watch a series or read a couple of
pages from a book that is level appropriate for you. But instead of just
consuming the information and forgetting it, you'll have to be intentional with
it here because your task is to retell what you have seen or read in your own
words. The story doesn't have to be long. It could be really short, maybe
just a minute or two. But you might need to write down some key points that you
want to talk about. Note the chronology of events or put down the vocabulary
that you want to use when you are retelling the story. This is a cool way
to push words from your passive vocabulary into your active one. It
forces you to actually use the language instead of just passively consuming it.
Another very important part of what I was doing when I was learning German is
grammar drills. Grammar might feel boring at times, but it's the foundation
of every language. So, having a resource for grammar drills, whether it's a hard

automatically without having to stop for an entire minute to think whether you
should say
orang. Finally, here's a fact that applies to any language learning system
and that we all really need to accept. Learning is always messy. It's a
nonlinear process with lots of ups and downs. And as much as we'd always like
to have a structured approach and steady progress, that's just not going to work.
You might think that once you have reached the B1 level, you will stop
making mistakes with things like word order or articles. But mistakes will
still happen, and that's okay. Developing your language skills is like
a stock market. It goes up and down, but the overall trend is upward hopefully,
and that's what matters. So, embrace the process, accept the messiness, and keep
going. [Music]

possibilities for forming longer sentences in German.
Sometimes, we need to give reasons or want to explain something in more
detail. In these cases, basic sentences aren't
enough. We need to combine two or more sentences
or use subordinate clauses. So, join me. So, we have already learned how to form
a basic sentence in German. So, if you still have any questions
related to the basic sentence, please check my video. Remember, the most important rule is
always the verb has to be in the second position.
We've also already learned how to connect main clauses.
We can use und oder or aber to do so.
Today, we will learn four more of these connectors.
But this time, it's a bit more difficult because we will have to change the verb
position. We will start with wenn. Wenn es regnet,
gehen wir ins Kino. So, you see we have a subordinate
clause. This is the wenn part. Wenn es regnet,
then we have to put a comma and then gehen wir ins Kino.
You can also see the first half of the sentence, the
subordinate clause, is ending with a verb
and the main clause begins with a verb. They are only separated by a comma.
It's really important you internalize this structure
because it's a common error to say "Wenn es regnet, wir gehen ins Kino."
But that's wrong. We have to put the verb first.
For the main clause, you can add dann. Wenn es regnet, dann gehen wir ins Kino.
This doesn't affect the verb position. You can add it, you don't have to.
This we can also flip this order around and start with the main clause.
Wir gehen ins Kino, wenn es regnet. So you see here we have the main clause
as a basic sentence, wir gehen in the second position, ins Kino.
Then we have to put the comma and the subordinate clause
begins with wenn. This pattern is very important.
You need it in a lot of situations and it will help you so much to vary
your sentence structure. So it's really essential if you want to
improve your speaking, writing, or even consider to take a test in German to
know these patterns. To now give you another example, wenn
ich später nach Hause fahre, nehme ich den Zug.
Or ich nehme den Zug, wenn ich später nach Hause fahre. The meaning doesn't change. It's up to
you which order you prefer in this case. Our second connector is dass.
You see we have to write two s and that really matters.
If it's only one s, it will be a relative clause
which we do later. So please don't mix those two up. So, you can see we have this short
introduction, which is typical for this das sentence. All of these expressions
then we have the comma and then das with double s.
And you see the verb goes to the very end of the sentence.
In our case, it's even two of them because we use the future tense.
And you see both verbs go to the end. Or we can use an additional modal verb. So, for this pattern, it's really
important you memorize the verb goes to the end of the sentence. Our third connector is obwohl, which
means even though. So, we have two sentences with kind of
opposite meaning. On the one hand, I like the summer. On
the other hand, I don't like high temperatures.
And we can perfectly connect such a sentence with obwohl.
You can see also in the case of obwohl, the verb goes to the end of the
sentence. And if we have two verbs,
both go to the end. >> So once again, both verbs go to the end.
The modal verb is in the last position. Our last connector is deshalb, which
means that's why. So we want to explain why we do
something. And we can combine those two sentences
with deshalb. You can see in this sentence
the verb doesn't go to the last position, but it follows deshalb
directly. So you can see here we have the verb
bracket. Only one verb goes to the end. The modal
verb is in the beginning. Or we can have a separable verb. Once again, this is the word order you
know from the basic sentence. The verb follows deshalb and the prefix
goes to the end of the sentence. I'll show you now a table with all the
four different connectors and how the verb position changes. It's really important to memorize these
rules and the one thing that will help you
to do so is practice. So we do a little exercise together. I hope this video has helped you. Thank
you so much for watching, and see you in the next one.

question. What is grammatical gender in German? In English, we use the word, the, for everything. For example, we say, the man, the woman, the house. But in German, there are three different
words that means the. And which one you use depends on the
grammatical gender of the noun. Now, in grammar, gender does not always mean male or female like in real
life. It is just a system in the language to
group nouns into three types. These three types are The word. Teer. is used for masculine nouns. The word. D is used for feminine nouns. and the Word. Thus, is used for neuter nouns. Let's look at some simple examples. The word for dog in German is... Hund It belongs to the masculine group, so we
say. der Hund This means the dog. The word for Lamp is... Lampe It is feminine, so we say. die Lampe This means the lamp. and the word for houses. House It is in the neuter group, so we say. das Haus This means the house. As you can see, each noun comes with a
special article that tells us the gender. It is very important to always learn the
article together with the noun. Do not just say. Hund or House Always say. der Hund, das Haus, ohr die Lampe Think of the article and the noun as one
word, like one full name. In German, this is not just a small
detail. It's a basic grammar rule. If you use the wrong article, your
sentence may sound strange or incorrect, but do not worry. You will learn to recognize the gender
over time. There are even tricks and patterns to help
you remember, and we will learn them later. Now that you know German has three
different words for The. And these are der, die and Thus, Let us learn how to actually use them with

As you know, German sentences are sometimes very long, and something that is particularly difficult for people learning German is finding out in which order to place different clauses one after another in a sentence. Today we'd like to introduce you to a rule that you may have already heard in (your) German lesson, and this rule helps you to find an order. This rule is called the "te-ka-mo-lo" rule. "Te-ka-mo-lo" stands for temporal, causal, modal and local. And now let's take a look at how it can look exactly. Let's go! Janusz? Oh, not now please. I'm filming my first TikTok video. And now we'll go through these different adverbial phrases - that's what you call these small parts of a sentence - step by step, and show you which order we use. Let's start with the "te" from our "te-ka-mo-lo" rule, and "te" stands for temporal, for time-based adverbs. In the sentence here it was the "currently". Janusz is currently filming. So this temporal phrase is always at the beginning of this information. And here you'll see another example with a temporal adverbial phrase. Oh, I've got three likes already since yesterday. I've got such a good feeling about it now. I've always known that TikTok is exactly my thing. In this example you saw that this temporal expression is always immediately after the conjugated verb. Today, I'm going to give you a very easy
language hack that'll help you to reset your German sentences. You can use this hack to help you get
around the more complicated German word order for now while you get used to
using the language. So, let's take a look at the kind of complicated German
word order that I'm talking about. Take a look at this sentence.
This word order is definitely different to how we'd say it in English. But, if it seems a little familiar to
you, it's probably because you've seen this word order used before by someone
kind of famous. When you think about this kind of
sentence structure as a form of Yoda speak, it doesn't seem that complicated.
But, never mind all that. We'll talk about
that another time. Today, we're talking about how you can avoid changing up your
word order altogether, and it's very easy. All you have to do is
hesitate. When Germans hesitate in natural
conversations, they essentially reset their sentence from the beginning. You

=== SOURCE #43 (ID: CxSs57hjOsI) ===
What if there was a secret German verb
form nobody ever taught you? A shortcut that lets you avoid tricky
verb changes like ich esse, but du isst. Well, there is at least something close
to that, and today I am letting you in on the secret.
In English, you constantly use forms like I am eating, she is sleeping, we
were watching. German technically does not have this
structure. It uses context or words like gerade to make clear something is
happening at that moment. For example, ich esse gerade. I am eating right now.
But, in spoken German, many people use a
structure that works very similar to the English continuous form.
And that is ich bin am [music] essen. So, literally, I am at the eating.
So, how does it work, and why is it easier to use?
Well, first of all, the structure itself is super, super easy.
>> [music] >> You use sein
plus am plus a nominalized infinitive. So, for
example, ich bin, so the conjugated verb of sein,
am arbeiten. I am working. Ich bin am
arbeiten. Or, as another example, you could also
say sie ist
am >> [music]
>> telefonieren. She's on the phone.
Sie ist am telefonieren. Or, one last example,
wir waren am diskutieren.
Wir waren am diskutieren. So, this time this is in the past tense, sein was
conjugated for the past tense, waren. We were discussing. Wir waren am
diskutieren. Now, the beauty is that you only need to
conjugate the verb sein, whether it's in the present tense or the past tense. And
you probably already know that one very well just because it's such an important
verb. No strong irregular verb changes anymore
like for essen. No surprises with the endings. All you need is sein plus am
and the verb but in the infinitive and with a capital letter because it's
nominalized. It's used as a noun. Super einfach, oder?
And it gets even better because this also works for the past tense. We saw it
in the example earlier. All you need to do is put sein in the imperfect.
To give you another example, we could also say
Wir waren den ganzen Abend am reden. So, we were talking. We're continuously
doing that the whole evening. Wir waren den ganzen Abend am reden.
Or one last example for the past tense, sie waren sehr lange am Aufräumen.
At the moment, this form is only recommended for spoken German and not
for written or formal language. However, it definitely has become a lot
more popular and widely used over the last year. And who really knows in which
way languages develop? Maybe in a few years' time, Duden will officially
recognize the am form even in written and formal language.
But before we finish, I want to show you a very similar form that you might have
come across before and that is actually already fully recognized in the German
language. Ich bin
beim Essen. So, again, literally I am at the eating or
I am eating. But, it uses beim instead of am. So,
what's the difference here? To be fair, there isn't very much of a
difference. I would use am plus the normalized verb to focus on the activity
itself, so on the eating. Or if you would use another verb as an
example, I could also say I am studying right now.
However, if I use beim, then it feels slightly more connected to
the to the event or to the situation or sometimes even to the place itself. So,
if I use arbeiten, I could say that means more like I'm at work,
referring to the place more than me doing some work right at that moment.
Again, the biggest difference right now is probably that while both versions are
constantly used in spoken German, the version with beim is the only one
that's officially recognized and you can use for written [music] and for formal
German. Also, don't forget the shortcut I'm
showing you today does not mean that you don't have to learn and master the
German verb conjugation rules anymore. It is really just a shortcut that works
if you're doing something right now or if you were doing something for some
time in the past. Okay?
Still need to learn your German verbs. Let's finish with two more everyday
examples where am is used quite a lot. I was cooking the whole weekend.
Or are you still traveling?
I hope this week's sneaky German hack was helpful to you.

=== SOURCE #44 (ID: 6EbDkKPVaac) ===
Hi everyone, this is Laura,
your German teacher here at MosaLingua. Welcome back to our series of
German grammar hacks. In this video, I am going to give you some simple
and yet powerful hacks to find the correct sentence order in German.
Let’s go! :) The first thing you should keep in mind whenever
you are constructing a sentence in German: it’s all about the verbs! Nn general,
German sentence structure is pretty flexible compared to many other languages.
But the position of the verbs is usually fixed. So my first hack is:
The conjugated verb always goes in the second position!
This is the case for all affirmative sentences. So let’s look at an example:
“Du gehst heute mit deinen Freunden ins Kino.” The conjugated verb is “gehst.” It is based on the verb “gehen” and
takes the ending “-st” in this case, because the subject is “du.” 
That's the second person singular. Are you ready to get on the German sentence structure rollercoaster? You could change this sentence to:
“Heute gehst du mit deinen Freunden ins Kino.” But also: 
“Mit deinen Freunden gehst du heute ins Kino.” Or: “Ins Kino gehst du heute mit deinen Freunden.” The first word or phrase tells you what the focus of the sentence is. And did you notice? “Gehst” always goes in the
second position – which doesn’t necessarily mean the second word (see the case of “Ins Kino,” which is a common sentence block because it expresses location!) – even though that means that you often place the personal pronoun “du”
behind the verb. This might sound strange at first, but is absolutely normal for native German speakers. Hack #2: If you have two parts of a verb form, the second part goes at the end!
For this hack, I am using a sentence in the “Perfekt” past tense. It is the tense you mostly use
when speaking about events that are finished. You can find all of the details about this tense in another video. For now, let’s concentrate on sentence structure.
Life without coffee is unimaginable, for me at least.
So I'd say: “Ich trinke jeden Morgen Kaffee.” And of course: “Ich habe auch heute morgen Kaffee getrunken.” Did you notice how “ich trinke” becomes
“ich habe … getrunken” in the past? Unlike many other languages, brutal German
splits up the two parts of the verb tense. “Habe” – the conjugated form – goes in the 2nd position, and the other part – “getrunken” goes at the end. This is also the case when you say: 
“Heute morgen habe ich auch Kaffee getrunken,” or “Kaffee habe ich auch heute morgen getrunken.” This applies to all verbs and verb tenses with two parts. If you use a modal verb
plus an infinitive, the separable verbs, the future tense, etc. Hack No. 3: In a a subordinate clause, the conjugated verb goes at the end. A subordinate clause introduces a second action, and therefore
a second verb that complements the main clause. One of the most important contexts is
explaining a reason. You would use the connector “weil.”
Let’s try it out: “Alex kann heute Abend nicht zu meiner Party kommen. Er
schreibt gerade seine Masterarbeit.” Connecting the sentence with “weil,”
you get: “Alex kann heute Abend nicht zu meiner Party kommen, weil er gerade seine
Masterarbeit schreibt.” Interesting! In order to stress that the second sentence
is “only” a subordinate clause, you put the verb – “schreibt” – at the end. The same goes for
when the sentence is in the past: “Alex konnte gestern Abend nicht zu meiner
Party kommen. Er hat seine Masterarbeit geschrieben,” which becomes “Alex konnte gestern
Abend nicht zu meiner Party kommen, weil er seine Masterarbeit geschrieben hat.”
The conjugated form “hat” now has the responsibility of ending the sentence.
Does this rule seem really confusing to you? Good news
– the last hack will simplify your life
when using subordinate clauses! Hack No. 4: Use the power of “Naja …!” This hack will prove especially useful to all beginners who are still struggling with
sentence structure. If you don’t feel confident enough yet to flip around verbs like pancakes, use this hack: the power of “Naja ...!” “Naja” is nothing but a filler
word, usually used when you need some time to think about what you want to say.
That’s exactly what we need. And the good thing is: it restarts
the sentence, so everything will follow the “normal” order.
Let me give you an example. Instead of saying “Ich lerne Deutsch, weil ich die
Sprache so schön finde.” You can simply say: “Ich lerne Deutsch, weil...
naja, ich finde die Sprache so schön!” You see? In the second example, we use
the “normal” structure after the comma: “Ich finde die Sprache so schön!” 
So, don’t use sentence structure as an excuse for not speaking…
As always, this is the most important advice: Start speaking as soon as possible, even though
your sentence structure is not always 100% correct. That’s all for today.
I hope these four hacks will help you see the light at the end of the
tunnel :) Have fun tossing and turning around the different parts
of your German sentences, but please: be gentle with the verbs! :)
Happy learning and see you soon!

=== SOURCE #54 (ID: aqLM9utTYCo) ===
Hello everyone and welcome to learn
German. In this video, we'll talk about seven common mistakes that all German
learners make and almost everyone makes mistakes when learning German. Here's
how to avoid them and learn smarter. Here's the first
one. Translating word for word. Trying to translate every sentence
word for word. German and English have different word orders, especially with
verbs. For example, I have eaten already is
wrong. My tip, learn full phrases or sentence patterns, not just words. Second mistake, skipping noun
genders. Ignoring noun genders. The thus articles are not optional. They are
crucial in German grammar. Always learn the article with the noun. Third mistake, avoiding speaking. When
you're only focusing on grammar and never
speaking. Many learners wait until they are ready, but no one ever feels fully
ready. Speak early and often, even to yourself. Mistakes are a part of
learning. Don't let fear stop progress. Fourth mistake, ignoring pronunciation
and skipping pronunciation practice. German has sounds that don't exist in
English. Mish, heta, uba, bza, words which have sh orl. Imitate native speakers. Record
yourself. Practice with minimal pairs. Don't make a long list of words. Just a
few should be enough to practice. Mistake number
five, learning vocab without context. When you're memorizing word lists with
no context, you're making a mistake. Isolated words don't stick. Learn
through themes, sentences, real life situations. For
example, instead of just drinking, learn instead of
just learn to instead of
just learn jazz music. Mistake six, not reviewing
regularly. Do you also make this mistake? Learning once and never coming
back to it. Without review, even well-learned material fades. Quick
quizzes, flashcards, or short rewatch sessions work
wonders. My tip, use spaced repetition, or regular mini reviews. Mistake seven, comparing progress to
others. Feeling behind because someone else learns faster. Everyone has a
different pace. Comparing leads to stress, not
success. Focus on your own journey. Even 5 minutes a day adds
up. Even 5 minutes a day add up. Celebrate progress, not perfection.
Remember, mistakes are a part of the journey. And now you know how to avoid
them. Got another mistake you've made or seen? Share it in the comments. By the way, if you're serious
about learning German, don't forget we offer completely free and well
structured courses for beginners and intermediate learners. You can learn at
your own pace, access plenty of materials, and join a community of real
success stories. Just check out the links below and start your journey
today. See you in the next lesson, and happy
learning. Tanga, thanks for watching this video. If you liked this video, do
give it a thumbs up and share it with your friends. Don't forget to subscribe
to the channel if you still haven't. You can click on the bell to receive
notifications whenever we upload a new video. If you have any comments or
questions, you can leave them in the comment section below. Choose our fetus

hello de Lana do you ever feel like
you're hitting a language learning block whenever you're trying to read in German
imagine this a flow of a good story is interrupted by the stumbling block of
unfamiliar words you spend more time in the dictionary than you do immersed in
the story you try to just power through but it makes no sense so you end up just
giving up on trying to read a book in German and you miss out on one of the
best ways to expand your vocabulary well fear not today we are diving into the
art of efficient reading for German Learners it's time to break through
those language barriers and enhance your reading experience ready to discover the
secrets to fluent reading without getting Lost in Translation Los Gates
problem number one stopping too much don't stop for every single word that
you don't know if you don't understand a word just keep going kind of try and
power through it a little bit and usually you'll figure it out based on
some context sometimes Clarity comes from just soldiering on if you
overanalyze every little thing you're going to end up just spending too much
time in the dictionary and not enough time in the story problem number two
overlooking or underestimating the power of cognates don't overlook or
underestimate the power of cognates in German if that word looks pretty similar
to what it is in your native language or man that looks like a word that I
already know it probably is take the German word Betha for example the word
in English is better these two are not perfect cognates to each other but if
you say them back to back fast enough eventually they sound pretty similar
Bessa better Bessa better Bessa better you get the idea here problem number
three not preading is there a vocab list that is provided by the author either at
the end of the chapter end of the page or at the end of the book well read
through that before you read the chapter if you familiarize yourself with this
list before you actually get started into the chapter your brain will
automatically already know those words when you come across them within the
context of the story it'll also help you to solidify the them in your brain so
that you actually remember the vocab when it's time for you to use it on your
own if you don't have a vocab list that is provided to you start with the back
of the book and you might be able to make up one of your own if you have one
of those books that says on the back of it that it's about this kind of a story
or this kind of category of thing or these are the places they're going to go
you can just look up those words that you'll probably need for those things if

getting awesome German learning resources when you use those links
you're also helping to support this channel by sending me a small commission
with no additional cost to you now for a bonus tip use the book to build your
vocabulary but don't let that process slow you down simply underline the words
that you come across that you don't know as you're reading don't go and look them
up right that second but just underline them while you're reading through the
chapter then once you're done with that chapter or that page or whatever your
goal for the day is whenever you get to the end of that section then go back
through the chapter and see what words you underlined come back and make a list
out of those things then you can go and look up the translations for each of
them once you have a list with the translations next to them you can reread

boost not only your comprehension of the story but also your retention of new

let's talk about cases. German has four of them. They show what role a word
plays in a sentence. We have the nominative case for the subject. As an

accusative case for the direct object as an example. I see the dog. And the dative case for
the indirect object like I give the dog a ball. and the genative
for possessions like this is the dog's ball. This one is rare

Next up, word order. German word order looks quite flexible, but it isn't
completely free. In a main sentence, the verb is always in the second position or different order, but the verb always
stays second. In yes and no questions and commands, the verb comes first.
For example, or commit. Commit.
And in subordinate clauses, the verb goes right to the end like in a vile in
a vile sentence. And now let's talk about verbs
themselves. We have regular verbs that follow a nice predictable pattern
like to learn. [Music] to write. And then we have irregular verbs. They like to change their vowels in the
do and cs form. For example,

you hear them. And for past tense, yes, many of our past tense verbs are
irregular, too. But it's better to pick them up naturally by reading and
listening to short stories and not trying to memorize endless verb lists.
And another thing German loves are compound words. Two or more words stuck
together to make something new. And here's a trick. The last word usually
tells you the main meaning and the article. And here are some examples.
Ram build the picture and ramen frame a picture frame. So build a ra towel. In my opinion, that's something that
makes learning German real fun. But what do you think? Do you find compound nouns
and compound words fun or annoying? Let me know in the comments below.
And a quick note on capitalization and pronunciation.
In German, all nouns are capitalized. Sounds may be a bit annoying, but it
makes reading much much easier. And here just a small input on pronunciation
after front vowels. So sounds where your tongue is pushed forward in the mouth
like in I it's pronounced as a and after back
vowels. So sounds where your tongue is pulled backward in the mouth like in
you feel your tongue in the back of your mouth. It's so and the German v
sounds like the English V like Vvasa vasa.
And if you struggle with making the German R sound in the throat, here's the
trick. Take a tiny tiny sip of water. Lean your head back slightly and gargle.
This helps you feel where the sound is produced. And afterwards, practice the R
sound with short words like or tiny sip. And let's quickly talk about the um in
German we have three and these can change the words meaning
completely here is an example means already. What is it already that late? But
or shun means beautiful. So keep that in mind when reading out loud or talking to
someone. Now, here's the good news. You already know much more German than you
think. Many English words come from German or share roots like
mother does house deer apple
to drink and de winter. So when you see familiar words just use them. It's
basically free vocabulary. And finally mistakes. Let's talk quickly
about making mistakes when learning German. First of all, they're normal.
Second, they're not proof that you're bad at German. They're actually proof
that you are learning. Your goal is to be understood.
And grammar details will improve the more you speak, listen, and read. And

=== SOURCE #65 (ID: fCYQ1WBTt-w) ===
Hello students. I'm Laura Bennett with German 
with Laura and in this series , Learning the German Language: The Essential Beginner's Guide, 
we're not going to go through lists of 'Zahlen', Farben', ' Obst', 'Gemüse', 
'Jahreszeiten', 'Monate'. I'm not going to hand you 'proverbial fish'. 
I want you to learn HOW to fish. I want you to learn how to learn the German language in 
the most efficient and effective ways possible. I've been teaching German for 15 years 
to students of all ages, all backgrounds, but there's been a common theme. They focus 
on building vocabulary for weeks, months, maybe years; finally traveled to a German-speaking 
country and they're so excited to put all of that vocabulary to use. but then they make the 
painful discovery that they don't have the tools to actually string those words together into 
sentences. Where did these students go wrong? Well, most of them are native English speakers who 
don't have experience learning foreign languages so they simply followed popular advice for how to 
go about learning German. So using popular apps, software programs, watching YouTube videos. But 
all of these resources, if not used correctly, will keep students relegated to what I 
call 'passive' and 'active' learning. These are low-efficiency and mid-efficiency ranges 
of learning, and if you really want to learn to speak German, that's not where you want to be. You 
need to be in this green zone of high-efficiency, 'applied' learning, and in this series I'm going 
to show you what it is about passive learning ,what it is about active learning that keeps 
you from achieving your goal. How you can take the same resources, the YouTube videos, the apps, 
the software programs, but use them differently so that you are in this zone of applied learning. 
After we examine those three types of learning we're also going to look at common questions 
that beginners have as they start learning the German language. Does the instant immersion method 
really work? Should I start speaking German right away? How fast can I learn German? Is translating 
good or bad? What's the best way to get started? Even if you've already been learning the 
German language for weeks, months, years, this series is still for you because you've most 
likely fallen into the common pitfalls of merely passive or active learning. If you are a complete 
beginner that's extra fantastic, because this series will help you save time and wasted effort 
when it comes to learning the German language. Okay, I know you are eager to get into the meat of 
this series. That starts at about a half hour in .If you want to, skip ahead, check that out, 
verify that this series is for you like i say it is, but then come back to this point. You need 
this first half hour of information ,as well. It is not enough to learn what to do, you have 
to also learn what NOT to do; what to stop doing so that you can learn to speak the German 
language in the best, fastest ways possible. In the next video we're going to dive into the 
details of what does passive, low-efficiency learning look like, and how can you avoid making 
those mistakes? The first type of learning that we need to avoid, because it is not going to help 
us achieve our goal of speaking German, is merely passive learning. This is low-efficiency learning 
where, sure, it's not a complete waste of time, but if we take that same amount of time and use 
it differently, use it more wisely, you can learn so much more and you can learn it better, and you 
can learn it faster. So what does low-efficiency, passive learning look like? It looks like 
sitting back, watching a lot of YouTube videos, nodding along video after video, maybe doing 
something else in the background. I know that you have the best of intentions. I know that you 
really want to learn. I also know that there are a lot of options out there and it's overwhelming 
to know where to start. This seemed like an easy way to at least do something. Okay, I get it. 
We are going to look at, a little bit later, how you can still use YouTube videos and learn 
with them. But just sitting back and watching them hour after hour, that is not going to help 
you speak German. There are two main pitfalls of working with YouTube videos. We need to be aware 
of them so we can avoid them. The first problem is that, generally speaking, all 
of the phrases and vocabulary lists are presented as isolated vocabulary words, 
like this. Okay, say you're watching a video learning items around the living room and on the 
list you learn 'Tisch' for 'table', 'Couch', which is obvious, and 'Kissen' which means 'pillow'. But 
this isn't enough information. You're not going to have a conversation with a German and point around 
the room demonstrating that you know how to say 'Tisch', 'Couch', 'Kissen'. You need more 
information in order to use this vocabulary. The first bit of information that is not 
optional, you must know this, is you have to know the gender of each of these nouns. Okay, this 
is a mind-blowing concept for us English speakers, but in German there are three genders. Every 
noun will have one of three genders: masculine, feminine or neuter, and here we have an example of 
each one. So 'Tisch', (table) is a masculine noun, and we would learn this by putting the word 
'der' in front of it and learning, specifically, 'der Tisch'. That's to help us remember 
that 'Tisch' is a masculine noun, because of that 'der' in front of it .And 
then in front of 'Couch' we would learn the word 'die' and that flags to us that 
'Couch' is a feminine noun. Finally, the word 'das' in front of 'Kissen' would tell us 
this is a neuter noun. So 'der die das' are all three different ways of saying the word 'the'. 
They all mean 'the', it's just a masculine way of saying 'the', a feminine way of saying 'the' 
and a neuter way of saying 'the'. I know, again, feels a little complicated, but it's absolutely 
essential. Now I know there are lots of YouTube lists that give you this information and that 
is great, but it's still not quite enough. You have to be able, with every new word that you 
learn ,you have to be able to plug it in to a complete sentence right away. You have to be able 
to practically use it in some way. For example, we need to be able to say 'der Tisch ist' (which 
means is) and then some sort of adjective such as 'neu'. 'Der Tisch ist neu.' The table is new, 
okay. Or with 'Couch' to say 'die Couch ist blau.' The couch is blue. Or with 'Kissen' 
to be able to say 'das Kissen ist weich' (it is soft ). In a little bit, I'm going 
to walk you through a step-by-step process so that you can write these short simple 
sentences yourself. So that you can find, you know, lists of vocabulary anywhere but 
actually be able to interact with them, apply that knowledge so that it will support 
you in achieving your goal of speaking German. But before we can get into those details, we still 
need to look at the second pitfall of working with just isolated vocabulary lists. The second pitfall 
of working with isolated vocabulary lists is that some vocabulary is irrelevant to you and your life 
and only you can make this decision. No one on YouTube can do this for you. There are two types 
of irrelevant vocabulary to be on the watch for. Firstly, we can have straight up 'wrong 
vocabulary' as in, if you don't talk about cars, then don't learn a lot of German vocabulary about 
cars. If you don't talk about music, you know, scales, arpeggios, chords, d minor versus d major 
etc., you don't need to learn that vocabulary in German either. You learn vocabulary that you 
are going to use. That might seem obvious, but the second type of irrelevant vocabulary 
trips up a lot of people, and that's learning good, useful vocabulary, that we're all reasonably 
going to use, but learning it at the wrong time. And the prime example of this type of irrelevant 
vocabulary is learning the German alphabet. I have heard so many students tell me that 
they think, "Oh,I need to learn the German alphabet first. One of the first things 
I should learn is the German alphabet." This is nonsense! You're not going to have a 
conversation with German, introduce yourself, and proceed to spell out your name, okay? You have 
to always think about the scenarios in which you would use given vocabulary and if you don't have 
all of the German that goes with that situation, then you're learning that vocabulary at the wrong 
time. So, for example, you need to be able to spell things out if you're on the phone making a 
hotel reservation or buying tickets for a concert, but until you're ready to do that, you probably 
don't need to know the German alphabet. You can continue working with YouTube videos of vocabulary 
lists, but you can take that passive learning and transform it into high-efficiency learning by 
simply interacting with those lists differently, like you got a glimpse of when we took 'Tisch, 
Couch and Kissen' and made complete sentences with them. I'm going to walk you through, step by step, 
the process of knowing how to do that on your own, but before we can talk about that, we need 
to address the mid-efficiency learning which is exactly where most students are. If you are 
working with a popular app or a software program then this is your category. And, again, we 
can take those same resources but transform how we interact with them, and be in the 
high-efficiency zone where you will learn to speak German in the best, fastest ways 
possible. Maybe you've fallen into that trap of the low-efficiency passive learning, 
working on irrelevant, isolated vocabulary. Maybe you'd already recognized the error of 
your ways and tried to upgrade to a better type of learning which is called active learning, and 
is the subject of this video. Active learning is middle efficiency level learning. It 
has some good aspects to it, but again, some pitfalls that you need to be aware of so that 
you can avoid them; so that you can be at this highest level efficiency learning, which will 
be the subject of the next video. But first, what does active learning look like? Many students 
who are at this middle range, active learning level are working with popular apps where you are 
identifying words, matching them up, unscrambling them. It's going to look something like this. 
You might have German words such as 'Frau, Junge and Mädchen'. You click on them to 
reveal the English translations, that, 'Frau' means 'woman', 'Junge' means 
'boy', 'Mädchen' means 'girl', okay? Then you interact with this information 
again by doing a matching exercise where now you have to pair up 'Frau' with 
its English translation; 'Junge' with its English translation; 'Mädchen' 
with its English translation. Then you get to these unscrambling activities where 
you have a complete sentence but it's all in a mixed up order and you have to click on the 
words in the right order in order to say, "I am a girl and you are a woman." 
'Ich bin ein Mädchen und du bist eine Frau." Active learning is attractive 
because it's fun to earn stars or hearts or diamonds or whatever. It's satisfying to learn 
German every day for a certain amount of time or have an unbroken streak or be able to level up 
through all of these stages in the program, but it's also deceptive because activity doesn't equal 
productivity. Many times it amounts to nothing more than busy work. I know that might sound a 
little offensive. I'm not saying it's nothing, but hear me out, because I'm trying to help. It's 
just like with the YouTube list scenarios. It's not inherently bad; it's not inherently wrong, 
but, passive learning--- it's low efficiency. It's low effectiveness. And active learning, it has its 
upsides but it's still not quite where you need to be. If all you want to know how to do is recognize 
German vocabulary, essentially be able to read in German, then active learning is probably enough 
for you. But if your goal is to speak German then this active style learning isn't going 
to take you there, because it's not setting you up to produce your own German. It sets you 
up to recognize German but not to create your own German utterances. That happens only in this 
next category of learning which we'll be talking about soon. So what is it about active learning 
that causes it to miss the mark? Where it falls short? The first problem with active learning 
is that it relies very heavily on translating. Now there are specific scenarios in which 
translation is necessary, and that's fine, but as a general principle, it's to be 
avoided. Why? It's going to slow you down, gum up the gears. It's a crutch. You're going to 
be dependent on it and you'll never be able to speak German any faster than you can translate 
in your head, which is pretty difficult to do in the middle of a conversation. So think about 
it like this. We have a triangle. The goal is the German word. There are two different ways 
to get there. There's the smart way and then there's the longer, harder way. So back up. 
How does this work with our native language? Without us consciously realizing this, we, when 
we were learning English, were thinking in images, sensations, and we were directly connecting 
that to the English word. So, for example, we would envision with our minds eye a table and 
we have the whole idea of a table: what a table looks like; how we use a table; the concept of a 
table. And we attached that to the English word 'table'. So now, you can do the same thing with 
German and that's what you want to do. You want to envision the concept of the word you're trying to 
learn and connect it directly to the German word, which in this instance is 'Tisch'. But what a 
lot of people do wrong with this active learning, the mid-level efficiency active learning, is 
that they go from the English word 'table' to the German word 'Tisch', but underneath the 
surface there's this whole other step there where they're still starting with the concept on 
a subconscious level but then feeding it through English to get to German as opposed to the desired 
route of directly from the concept to the word. So now we've already covered the dangers 
of learning an isolated German word along with its English translation, but the thing 
is that even learning entire sentences with their English translations is not going to help you. 
You're not going to learn a sentence like this: 'Ich sehe den Mann, die Frau und das Baby' 
and its English translation of 'I see the man, the woman, and the baby.' You're not going to 
memorize these kind of sentences hoping to have a perfect situation in which to use them when you 
go to Germany. We don't speak English by parroting memorized phrases and so you shouldn't be trying 
to learn German that way either. But looking at these sentences gets at the second inherent 
problem with this mid-level efficiency, active learning, and that is that all of the 
grammar pieces are included for you, but they're not being explained. For example, we have this 
now familiar sentence 'Ich sehe den Mann, die Frau und das Baby' (I see the man, the woman, and the 
baby). Now check out the second sentence. To our English brains, the only thing that we're changing 
is what we're doing. Now we're no longer SEEING the man, the woman, and the baby; we are HELPING 
them. 'Ich helfe dem Mann, der Frau und dem Baby.' But observe all of the additional changes 
that are happening in German. We have 'Mann' in both sentences but the way 
we're saying the word 'the' in front of 'Mann" is changing. Here it is 'den' and here it 
is 'dem', right? We have the same situation with the word 'Frau.' It occurs in both sentences. Both 
times it translates to 'the woman' and yet here in German it is 'die Frau' and yet when we're helping 
her now it is 'der Frau'. Okay, we have the same problem with baby. We see the baby. We help the 
baby. But the way we're saying 'the' changes from 'das' to 'dem'. So not only is this just 
further evidence of the dangers of translating, (they're leaving out a lot of information that 
you need to know; just that simple translation is not enough) but again, getting at a second 
problem here, which is that all of these grammar changes that are just being included for you are 
not being explained. And you have to understand them if you want to speak German. You have to 
not only understand them, you have to be able to make these changes yourself. The good news is, 
this is possible with our high level efficiency learning which is what I call 'applied learning' 
and that is the subject of our next video. We've looked at the details of passive learning, 
active learning and how the lists on YouTube, unscrambling sentences with 
popular apps and software programs, they're not inherently bad or wrong it's 
how we're interacting with them that makes for low-efficiency learning or mid-efficiency 
learning. We can take those same resources and interact with them differently, and that puts 
us into the green zone of high-efficiency applied learning. If you want to learn how to 
speak German, this is where you need to be. Both passive and active learning 
focus just on building vocabulary, but this is just half the story and the other 
half is grammar. If you don't understand German grammar, you can't string vocabulary 
together and actually use it. We need to be at this place where vocabulary and 
grammar overlap; they interact. It doesn't matter if you've been working with 
thematic vocabulary lists on YouTube or elsewhere, or if you've been matching or unscrambling or even 
memorizing your complete sentences. The grammar is either missing entirely or it's not being 
explained and we have to understand the grammar in order to use it, of course, and it's when we 
combine vocabulary and grammar that we're in that green zone of high-efficiency learning. So 
I'm going to give you a strategy, a formula, for writing these short simple sentences, which I 
call sentence drills. You need to be able to take vocabulary, regardless of where you're getting it 
from: a YouTube video or an app software program; whether it's in list form already or it's embedded 
in an entire sentence, and you have to pull it out. You need to take this vocabulary, right from 
the beginning, add on the grammar and be able to compose complete sentences. We can take those 
resources from passive and active learning but transform them into applied learning by doing this 
intentional interaction with them. That's what makes it high efficiency. That's how you can learn 
better and you can learn faster. First, we'll look at some examples of different kinds of sentence 
drills. Then, we'll walk through a few practice examples, and finally, I will show you an optimal 
way to set up a whole exercise of sentence drills that you can do in rapid fire fashion. So let's 
say that you want to watch a YouTube video on basic fruits and vegetables. The first three words 
that come up are 'der Apfel' (apple), 'die Banane' (banana) and 'das Gürkchen' (little cucumber). 
So, at this point, stop the video! Don't keep watching! You need to intentionally interact 
with this new vocabulary. You need to apply it by putting it into a sentence drill like these. 
For each of those isolated vocabulary words, we can then follow up with the word 'ist' (which 
means simply 'is') and then after that with an adjective, which notice I'm keeping that the 
same, too. 'Lecker'means 'delicious'. So we have 'der Apfel ist lecker,' 'die Banane 
ist lecker,' 'das Gürkchen ist lecker.' One at a time we can change each of these 
four components. So, now, in these sentences, right, we're always talking about the banana, 
the banana, the banana and it's delicious, delicious, delicious. And what's changing? The 
first word is changing. Instead of saying 'die Banana' every time (the banana), here we're saying 
'diese Banane' (this banana), 'Jede Banane' (every banana) and then 'welche Banane ist lecker' (Which 
banana is delicious)? We can also change the verb component to our sentence. We can say 'war' for 
'was', 'ist' for 'is', 'wird' for 'will be'. Or finally we can change that fourth 
component, the adjective. We can say 'die Banane ist weich' (it's soft); 'die Banane 
ist gelb' (it's yellow); 'die Banane ist süß' (it's sweet). So now the exact formula that we're 
going to look at in this video, that I'm gonna walk you through is this one. 'Die kleine Banane 
ist lecker.' 'Die große Banane ist lecker.' 'Die reife Banane ist lecker.' The small banana 
is delicious. The big banana is delicious. The ripe banana is delicious. So I've added 
in here a second adjective. We have not only the ones at the end of the sentence, but we also 
have one in between the word 'the' and then the noun itself, so that the formula looks like 
this. 'The' and then adjective, whatever noun, the word 'ist' for is and then another adjective. 
Okay. so before you stop this video thinking, "Great! Got it. I can do sentence drills now. 
We'll just use the word for 'the' and then pick out an adjective and pick out a noun and then 
'ist' and then another adjective. Got it." No, you don't, because there's more grammar happening here 
than you might realize at this point. For example, all of these 'dies' mean 'the', but so does the 
word 'der', and then also the word 'das' and 'den' and 'dem' and 'des'. All six of these words 
mean 'the' in German. It's a grammar thing .But you have to understand it. You have to be able to 
use it correctly in order to speak German. And, in the same vein, we have slightly different ways 
to say all of these adjectives. It's ending with an 'e' here, like this version of 'the', but we 
can have our adjectives end with an 'r' or an 's' or an 'n' or an 'm' as well. And, again, 
there are particular reasons why. There are principles and patterns that dictate which 
changes we make to which words and when and how, and you ultimately have to know all of this in 
order to speak German. And the time to start with it is now. And I will show you the most basic 
place to start. Okay, so now, to run through the process of writing your own sentence drills,

impact exactly which version of 'the' we're going to use and which version of our adjectives big 
and small and ripe and whatever. The gender of the noun, it plays a role in that, so you have to know 
this. Wherever you are learning German, if you're learning nouns of any kind and the 'der die das'

it's masculine, feminine, or neuter-- just like that piece of the puzzle impacts exactly how we 
say 'the' and exactly how we say our adjectives, so, likewise, the role that the noun is playing 
and therefore the case that it is in is also going to impact exactly how we say 'the' and 
similar words: 'this', 'that', 'my', 'his', etc. and also how we say our adjectives. So this 
means that for every German noun that you learn, you need to learn its gender along with 
it, whether it's masculine with 'der', feminine with 'die', neuter with 'das'. But then, 
you also have to know what role it's playing in the given sentence. You have to know whether it's
