Hello students. I'm Laura Bennett with German with Laura and in this video we will address the 
question: Is the instant immersion method the best way to learn German? This video is part of 
a larger series 'Learning the German Language: The Essential Beginner's Guide,' but 
this video does also stand on its own .So so many students think that the best, fastest 
way to learn German would be to go to Germany and instantly hear only German and read only German 
and speak only German. Or if you can't actually travel right away, to duplicate this at home. Take 
an instant immersion course or class where all of the explanations, even, are in German and you're 
listening to German music and you're watching German shows and you're reading German books. 
German, German, German. Does this really work? I want you to think about a 
child learning how to swim. Can we throw that child into the ten foot and 
say, "Hey, learn how to swim. You can do it." Sure, we could, and maybe that poor child will 
flutter and sputter and flail around enough that they can keep their head above water. They 
can survive barely by the skin of their teeth. But what would be the better way to actually learn 
how to swim? You have to start off in the kiddie pool. You have to take some lessons. You have to 
learn some techniques. You have to have someone who shows you how to swim and then you will be 
prepared to go out into the deep end and succeed at swimming instead of potentially drowning. 
I've known people who have gone to Germany not speaking a lick of German and they didn't come 
out of it fluent in German. No, instead they ended that experience feeling overwhelmed, frustrated, 
and knowing a handful of broken German phrases. So what does it look like to start off in the 
kiddie pool and learn the essentials that then set us up for success here in the deep end? We 
have to hit three benchmarks: we need to learn the right thing at the right time and in the 
right way. There's this fancy phrase in the linguistics world that when you're learning 
German or any other language, you need to be focused on 'comprehensible input plus one', but 
what this means is that the majority of the German that you're hearing or reading, it needs to be 
understandable to you and you should be stretched just a little bit so that you're constantly 
improving. But if it's 'comprehensible input plus 10' or 'comprehensible input plus 100', that's too 
much. You're going to be flooded with all sorts of information that you don't have the tools to 
digest. When you first start off learning German in the kiddie pool, there are specific vocabulary 
words and specific grammar structures that you need to focus on first. You have to build the 
right foundation. This would include, for example, about 2,000 words of basic vocabulary. 
We'll look at the details in a second. Then, as far as grammar is concerned, you need to have 
a foundational understanding of the nominative, accusative, and dative cases. Again, examples 
coming soon. You need to learn the three most common verb tenses that you will use the most 
and you also need to learn what is called the subjunctive mood. When it comes to the 2,000 
word basic vocabulary, I want you to think of a pyramid. The foundation of it is going to be a 
bunch of basic noun categories such as clothing, foods, furniture, professions, places, etc. After 
that we have a bunch of verbs that we need to work on, about 50 common verbs, and you need to think 
in terms of learning the verb 'to eat' but saving verbs such as 'to nibble', 'to snack', 'to munch', 
etc. for down the road. But then you also need to learn what are called 'modal verbs' and verbs 
with 'separable prefixes'. Then, after that, we need to add in a few dozen basic adjectives and 
then a handful of other types of words --adverbs and prepositions. In order to become proficient or 
even fluent in German you have to, from the very beginning, start understanding the German system 
for indicating who's the subject of the sentence; who's the direct object of the sentence; who's 
the indirect object of the sentence. For example, to say 'Der Mann bacht', (the man bakes). He's 
the subject of the sentence. That means he's in the nominative case. In this next example, he's 
still the subject; still in the nominative case, but now we're talking about WHAT he's baking, 
and that 'what' (a cake) is the direct object, which in German goes into the accusative case. In 
this third example, 'Der Mann backt seiner Frau einen Kuchen', our man is still the subject, still 
in the nominative case. The cake is still what he's baking so it's still in the accusative case, 
and the person for whom the cake is being baked (his wife) is in the dative case because she's the 
indirect object in this sentence. And I know that this is super overwhelming, okay? I have a free 
mini-course called 'Unlocking German Grammar'. You can click on the link down below, open that 
up in a new tab and register for that course today so that you can start better understanding 
what on earth I was just talking about here. But in this video, suffice it to 
say, you need to learn this stuff! Okay, so we need to work on a basic vocabulary 
of 2,000 words. We need to learn the nominative, accusative, and dative cases. We also need to 
learn the three most basic verb tenses that you're going to use the most. The first is the present 
tense, as in 'Der Mann backt.' The man bakes. Second is the past tense 'Der Mann backte.' The 
man baked. Third is another past tense that German uses even more often. 'Der Mann hat gebacken.' 
which is essentially another way of saying 'the man baked'. The fourth and final element 
of kiddie pool German that you need to build first is working on what is called the subjunctive 
mood, and that would mean, for example, knowing how to say 'Der Mann würde backen', 
(the man would bake if dot dot dot) or 'Der Mann hätte gebacken, wenn...' (then the man would 
have baked if) or saying 'Der Mann wäre Bäcker, wenn...( the man would be a baker if). Once you 
build up these basic techniques in the kiddie pool, then you are ready to head out into the 
deep end and experience full immersion of taking in the German left and right, speaking only in 
German, asking questions when you don't understand something, building vocabulary like a madman 
every day. You're ready for that when you have the foundation first. Instant immersion is a recipe 
for drowning. But full immersion, right, the right thing at the right time, done in the right 
way because you have the kiddie pool foundation, is fantastic,.But be sure to follow my advice 
of the vocabulary and those specific grammar points in the kiddie pool so you can be set up for 
success in the deep end. All the misinformation on the instant immersion method ties hand in 
hand with another prevalent belief which is, if you want to speak German, the best way 
to get started is to simply start speaking German. Does this really work? We're going to 
look at that in the next video. Hello students. I'm Laura Bennett with German with Laura and in 
this video we want to answer the question: How soon am I ready to have a conversation in German? 
This video is part of a larger series 'Learning the German Language: The Essential Beginner's 
Guide' but this video does also stand on its own. There's a massive misconception out there 
that if you want to learn to speak German, the best way to get started is to simply start 
speaking. This idea is very much so connected to the other misconception that we've talked about 
already, that the instant immersion approach is the best way to learn German. But just like 
we saw in the video on that misconception, giving students the advice to simply start 
speaking German from the very beginning is like throwing someone who can't swim into the deep 
end and saying, "Good luck! You'll figure it out." Are there people who flap and flail around 
enough that they keep their heads above water, maybe even get to that point that they do, 
on their own, figure out certain swimming techniques? I suppose so, but, in my book, this is 
going about learning to speak German the hard way. The most efficient and effective way to learn 
to speak German is to not throw yourself into it and try to figure it out as you go along, 
but start at the beginning, in the kiddie pool, and have a step-by-step process, a system for 
getting yourself to that place that you can successfully swim in the deep end. You do want 
to start speaking German from the very beginning, but there's a massive qualifier to that statement. 
There are two different types of speech, what I call 'scripted speech' and 'created 
speech'. Created speech is the 'jumping into the deep end before you know how to swim'. This 
is where you're thinking up your own sentences of how to introduce yourself and talk about your 
hobbies etc. Scripted speech, on the other hand, is already written for you. This is like 
taking a German song that you really like, finding the lyrics, and singing along to it. Or 
finding an easy beginner German reader, getting the book, also getting the audio book, and then 
reading it aloud along with the audiobook version. It's scripted because everything's taken care of 
for you. You don't have to worry about vocabulary that you don't yet know. You don't have to worry 
about grammar structures that you don't yet know. It's all done for you, which frees 
you up to focus on getting your body used to making new German sounds. There are 
letters of the alphabet like the umlauted 'a', 'o' and 'u' that make sounds we're not used 
to in English. You need to practice these and you need to practice overall inflection so 
that you're not speaking in a really choppy, hesitant way. You also want to develop as 
authentic of an accent as possible. You don't want to sound like this: (bad German accent). 
You want to sound like a German: (good German accent). Big difference, big difference. You 
can get there, but starting with scripted speech is key. You need to hear lots of 
authentic German and practice matching it long long long before you are ready to start 
making your own German utterances. There's a step-by-step process for you here. So, as already 
mentioned, you start with something such as listening to music. Then that's going to lead you 
to the place that after building basic vocabulary, learning essential grammar structures, you put 
that into practice by creating your own sentence drills. This is a written exercise where you 
get to practice utilizing new vocabulary and the grammar that helps you string it all together. 
But after you write these sentence drills you can also read them aloud and get more scripted speech 
practice. From there, you would further expand into writing entire dialogues. You imagine 
yourself in a situation at the grocery store, at the bank, at the post office, and write out, 
again, written exercise. Write out--What would you say? What might someone else say to you? Then 
what would you say? You play act, essentially, through these scenarios and that will help you 
recognize where you might still have some holes in your basic knowledge, where there are some new 
vocabulary words that you still need to learn, or basic grammar structures that you 
still need to learn. Okay, from there, (because again you can read these dialogues out 
loud and that's more scripted speech practice after you have some of this under your belt), the 
next step is to find at least one native speaker, ideally who also is a good teacher. It's not 
enough to just be a native speaker. You have to find someone who will correct you when you make 
mistakes, right, every time you make a mistake, because that's what you need--someone who 
will correct you and who can also explain why something is the way it is. That's the ideal 
scenario with this person. Then you can start having targeted conversations. You can start 
with a mix of the scripted and created speech. You can prepare a topic that brings us back to 
the idea of dialogues. You can prepare a topic in written form but then have the conversation 
where you're not reading from the dialogue, you're just trying to draw upon that practice in order to 
be more successful in that conversation. After you get some practice with that trusted teacher, that 
trusted guide, who is further honing your skills, then you are ready for going out into the wild and 
having as many conversations as you possibly can, because at this point you've built up a certain 
amount of comfort, a certain amount of confidence, because you've gone through a step-by-step process 
that is systematically preparing you to create your own German utterances. So at this point I can 
hear you ask the question: How long? How long will this process take? That is the subject of our next 
video: How fast can I learn to speak German? Hello students. I'm Laura Bennett with German with Laura 
and in this video we want to look at the question: How fast can I learn the German language? This 
video is part of a larger series 'Learning the German Language: The Essential Beginner's Guide', 
but this video does also stand on its own. Before we can answer that question, we have 
to first look at a related question which is: What does it mean to speak German? Or what does 
it mean to be proficient or even fluent in German? And there are a lot of misconceptions around this 
topic. The first thing that you need to understand is that there are two types of vocabulary. 
I want you to picture with me an iceberg. Only a little bit of the iceberg is above the 
surface of the water and the majority of it is underneath the surface. That part 
of the iceberg is 'passive vocabulary' and above the surface of the 
water is 'active vocabulary'. What this means is that you will understand 
a lot more vocabulary than you use. And this will also be true as you learn to speak German. 
So not only should you reasonably expect that you will understand a lot more German than you 
actually use yourself in writing or in speaking, but this gets even better yet, because the 
definitions of fluency in a foreign language are that even when you have a third of the 
vocabulary of a native speaker, you're still considered fluent. So basically, your iceberg 
is a third of the size of a native speaker's and yet that's still considered fluent. Another 
concept that's very important to keep in mind as you're building your German iceberg of vocabulary, 
is that, just like you're never gonna know all the words that exist in the English language, 
you're never gonna know all of the words that exist in German either. Neither do native German 
speakers. And you need to be really focused on the vocabulary that you're actually going to use. 
If you are a professor and you give all sorts of academic talks, you're going to have to know very 
specialized vocabulary. If you are a car mechanic, that's other specialized vocabulary. If you are a 
doctor, a musician, a lawyer, and you want to be able to talk about those same things in German, 
that's a lot of very specialized vocabulary. But we don't have to learn all of those words if 
they don't apply to us. So, it's really important, in this larger conversation of how fast can 
I learn to speak German, that you think about the life that you lead; the words that you use 
in English; the topics that you talk about in English. And make sure that you are focusing your 
time on learning those same words in German. Now, in previous videos on other misconceptions about 
how to learn German, we've talked about how it's actually really inefficient and ineffective to 
throw yourself into the deep end and try to figure out the German language as you go along. That 
you'll actually save yourself a lot of time, not to mention frustration, if you appropriately start 
off in the kiddie pool and step by step gradually build up to being out in the deep end. And that 
important truth ties us into the very common misconception that adults learn more slowly than 
children; that it's going to take adults longer to learn a foreign language than a child would do 
it. And studies have actually shown that when you look at the number of hours put into learning a 
language, adults can learn infinitely faster than children can and that's partly because children, 
in a way, have no choice but to be thrown into the deep end and learn how to sink or swim. 
And adults, in contrast, we can take this very analytical approach. We can have a step-by-step 
technical process for learning language which is much, much faster. We had to do the deep 
end approach when we were learning our native language, (that's what everyone has to do), but 
since we have our native language under our belt we can now apply our understanding of grammar 
structures and just our vocabulary that we already have, we can use all of that to our advantage to 
learn German much, much faster than a child can. So now, to really black and white answer this 
question: How long is it going to take?How quickly can I be fluent in German? Of course, there's 
not going to be a one-size-fits-all answer, but I can say that it's all about how you go about 
it. If you know how to intentionally practice; if you make sure that you're in the zone 
of high-efficiency applied learning, which we talked about earlier in this series, you will 
learn the fastest possible. And it could take you a matter of months, maybe even weeks. It's about 
how many hours you can put in in a given day, in a given week. But if you do it right, it 
doesn't have to take you years before you're having those desired conversations in German. So, 
now, there are four key impediments to learning German as quickly as possible. We've talked about 
two of them already in previous videos and those are the misconceptions that using the instant 
immersion method is a good idea and that speaking right away, creating your own German utterances 
right from the beginning, is an efficient process. it's not. So now we have two final impediments 
or hindrances that we need to be aware of so we can avoid them; so that you can learn to speak 
German as quickly as possible. In the next video we're going to look at the question: When is it 
appropriate to translate? and when not? When is it going to help you? And when is it getting in 
the way? Hello students. I'm Laura Bennett with German with Laura and in this video we want to 
look at the question: Is translating good or bad? This video is part of a larger series 'Learning 
the German Language: The Essential Beginner's Guide', but this video does also stand on its 
own. In order to answer this question, I need you to look at this language triangle with me. The 
top of the triangle is our goal--learning German words. There are two types of vocabulary that we 
need to build and two different ways of building it. The first type is concrete vocabulary, which 
is anything that we can see, hear, taste, touch, smell. This vocabulary we can build by connecting 
directly from the concept of, what that thing is, to the German word. For example, we can see 
a picture of something like this (a flower) and we can directly associate that with the 
German 'die Blume. That's what this is--'die Blume. That's how we build all concrete 
vocabulary: foods, furniture, clothing, places. Anything that you can see, hear, taste, touch, 
smell, you should be learning this way, right, by connecting directly from the concept to 
the German. That's going to be the fast way to learn German and that's the only way that true 
fluency in German is possible. You don't have time in a conversation to translate every word in your 
head. You don't want to do that. And if you follow this process; of this particular way of building 
concrete vocabulary in German, you won't get stuck in that trap. You'll be able to think quickly 
in German and find all the words that you need. But there's a second type of vocabulary, which is 
the opposite of concrete vocabulary, and that is abstract. These are words such as: freedom, 
friendship, kindness, economics. We don't have a way to touch economics. We can't smell 
freedom. We can't taste friendship. These are abstract nouns and we have to learn these 
differently. This is where translation DOES come into play. You have no choice but to translate 
from English, or whatever your native language is, into German. It's a long way, because 
we're essentially going from the concept, through our native tongue, and then 
translating into German. So when you initially learn an abstract word you have to do this, 
but we need to get away from translating as soon as possible and the way that we can do 
that is by taking all of those new words: freedom, kindness, friendship, whatever it is, 
and immediately applying them into sentence drills which I talked about earlier in this series. You 
have to use the vocabulary in an applied way, right away, and if you do that, you'll be able to 
get away from the perpetual translation of those words and you will still internalize the German 
subconsciously and be able to draw upon that rapidly just like a native speaker. So translation 
isn't inherently good or bad. It can be the right thing if it's the right time and it's used in the 
right way. So now we have one final misconception that we need to correct so that you can be well 
on your way to learning German as efficiently and effectively as possible. In the next video, we're 
going to look at the question: Is learning German all a matter of building as much vocabulary 
as possible? Or is there something else to it? Hello students. I'm Laura Bennett with German 
with Laura, and in this video we want to address the misconception that learning to speak the 
German language is a simple matter of building vocabulary. That, whatever you want to say, you 
take that English word, you learn the German equivalent, you string them all together like 
we would in English, but now, presto, chango, you're speaking German. This video is part of 
my larger series, 'Learning the German Language: The Essential Beginner's Guide', but this video 
does also stand on its own. There are two sides to learning in German or any other language. You 
have to learn vocabulary words, but then you also have to learn the grammar rules which allow you to 
correctly string those vocabulary words together and say something. You're speaking German in 
this place where vocabulary and grammar overlap. One on their own without the other is not enough. 
There are so so many students who think that they have to build at least some vocabulary first 
before they start learning any grammar at all, and this is the exact opposite of the truth which 
is, you actually need to start with grammar. It's so tempting to think, "Okay, I'll just 
build up some basic vocabulary. You know, I'll learn 'Tisch', you know, for 'table', 'Couch' 
obvious, 'Kissen' which means 'pillow'. You know, I'll just learn some stuff like that then I'll 
work on the grammar so that I can say, you know, the table is brown and the couch is blue and the 
pillow is green. But I'll learn some of the vocab first. That'll be better." No! Because in German, 
even when you're just learning some basic nouns like this, whether it's furniture or clothing 
or foods or plants or what have you, you already have to learn some grammar encoded along with them 
because you have to know the gender of every noun in German when you learn it. You have to know that 
it's not just 'Tisch', it's 'der Tisch'. This is a masculine noun.(der) You have to know that 'Couch' 
is a feminine noun. It is 'die Couch'. You have to know that 'Kissen" is a neuter noun; that it is 
'das Kissen'. The very first thing that you should learn before you start learning any vocabulary 
is you should study noun endings and noun groups. Noun endings are particular ways that nouns are 
spelled in German that are associated clearly with one gender over the others. So, for example, any 
time that you see the ending 'ling' on a German word, you can know that that word is a masculine 
noun. It's going to have a 'der' in front of it--'der Feigling', 'der Lehrling', etc. Then, so 
likewise, if you see the ending 'keit', you can know, automatically, that whatever word is ending 
with 'keit' is a feminine noun. 'Freundlichkeit, for example--friendliness. That's 
a feminine noun because it ends with 'keit' so it has to have a 'die' 
in front of it--'die Freundlichkeit'. All right, then finally, so likewise, there 
are lots of neuter endings. For example: ment. 'das Experiment', 'das Instrument'. Nouns that end with 'ment' are, as a rule, 
neuter nouns and learning all of these endings saves you so much time it's unbelievable. 
For example, just going back to the 'keit' here. If you remember that the 'keit' ending 
is feminine, then you will automatically know the gender of literally hundreds of nouns. 
So now it's also really powerful to learn noun groups and their associations with one gender 
over the other two. For example, weather elements, as a rule, are masculine nouns. Whether you're 
saying 'der Wind' for 'wind'; 'der Frost' (frost); 'der Hagel' for 'hail', okay, weather elements are 
usually masculine nouns. Trees, fruits and flowers are usually feminine nouns. And then an example 
of neuter nouns would be the elements of the periodic table--so various chemicals and minerals 
and stuff like that are usually neuter nouns. So if you're first armed with this information 
on noun endings and noun groups and THEN you start building up some basic vocabulary, 
you will immediately be able to practice solidifying the information on the different noun 
endings and the noun groups when you learn those words. You'll learn the WHY behind them. You won't 
just learn 'der die das' in front of the noun, (you'll learn that, sure) but then you'll be able 
to say, "Oh, yes, and it's a masculine noun." It's 'der' whatever whatever because of its ending. 
Or it's 'der' because it belongs to a particular group ,so on and so forth. And when you know that 
'why', that sticks in your brain so much better. 'Der die das' is not just random. There are 
patterns; there are principles. And you need to know those before you waste any time trying 
to learn vocabulary without them. Trying to learn just that random 'der die das' which is really 
hard for our brains to hold on to. So now, I have a whole German grammar course called 
'German Foundations' which is full of all of the essential grammar topics that I believe you should 
study first, before you start building vocabulary, so that you are well prepared with the necessary 
grammar to then use that vocabulary right away, which again, we've talked about this earlier 
in this series, that whole concept of sentence drills. So you can sign up for that, 
of course, but there's actually one step earlier than my 'German Foundations' course and 
that is a free mini-course that I've put together for you called 'Unlocking German Grammar'. 
You really should start there. It'll help you fully reap all of the benefits of 'German 
Foundations' if you do. So if you haven't already, click the link in the description below 
and sign up for 'Unlocking German Grammar' right now and get started with that free material. 
Thank you for learning German here with me on YouTube. it's been a pleasure and I hope to see 
you in my course 'Unlocking German Grammar' next.

=== SOURCE #70 (ID: J1EhsIacwEg) ===
These are my five tips that I would give myself if I wanted to learn German in the shortest possible time. Ready? So, thumbs up and let 's get started. Number one. Don't learn words. I once tried to learn one language for years with poor results. Then I changed just one thing and learned seven languages ​​ much faster and much easier. What is this thing? I'll explain now. I stopped memorizing vocabulary lists and started learning and understanding entire useful phrases. Thanks to this, I was able to communicate with Germans very quickly in typical everyday situations in a shop, restaurant or bank, and as a result, I was able to simply converse in German and become more fluent in speaking. Thanks to such complete sentences and phrases, I also naturally acquired grammar without having to memorize tables and rules. Number two. This way you will remember it easily and for a long time, even if you have a poor memory. I used to think I had a bad memory because I kept forgetting words in German. However, I found a way to remember it easily and for a long time. And this simple method works not only for me, but also for people of all ages, including my students who only started learning German after retirement. This method is to use so-called interval repetitions. Interval repetitions are repetitions that we do at continuously increasing intervals. This means that we do the first repetition after one day, then after two, four, 8, 16 and so on. This is a scientifically proven way to memorize, thanks to which you will learn less on the one hand , and remember things for longer on the other. And the best thing is that nowadays you have to arrange such repetitions. themselves . For example, in our Language in 15 Minutes app, artificial intelligence will automatically arrange interval repetitions for each of you. The link to the app can be found under this recording. And here comes tip number three. Learn a language less, but more often. Imagine you want to boil water. You put the kettle on the stove for one minute, then take it off the heat for six minutes, then put it on the heat again for one minute and take it off for six minutes. This way you could try to bring water to a boil for years, but never actually boil it. This is exactly how your German learning will look like if you study only one day a week and do not touch the language for the remaining six days . There is a proverb: you can even eat an elephant. Just eat it piece by piece. It's the same with language. Instead of learning a lot at once in one day, it is much better to learn in smaller portions, regularly, every day, piece by piece. And that's how I learned all the languages ​​ I know. Number four. Stop taking listening tests. One of the most stressful things I remember from my German lessons at school was the listening tests , where once a month the teacher would play us audio in German and we had to complete AC tests with the answers to what was said. These types of tests are absolutely useless when it comes to language learning. Fortunately, if you have difficulty understanding Germans by ear, if you think they speak too fast, there is one simple solution. Listening comprehension is like a diet. Just by standing on the scale several times a month, i.e. testing our weight, we certainly won't lose weight. To lose a few kilos, all you need to do is stick to your diet every day . It's similar with learning a language. To start easily understanding German by ear, all you need to do is listen to German regularly, preferably every day or every two days . At the beginning, it is best to listen to the phrases and sentences you are learning and whose vocabulary you already know. This will help you stop translating in your head and teach your brain to understand German. And advice number pi: talk as much as possible. You can't learn to swim from books first and only then get into the water, because we learn to swim by swimming. Similarly, it is impossible to learn a language first and then start speaking. Because just like swimming, we learn by swimming. In the same way, we learn to speak a foreign language by speaking. And when you start speaking, don't worry about the fact that you need more time at the beginning to build a sentence, choose the right words, or that you make mistakes, because everyone makes them. After all, even babies learning to walk do not start by running immediately, but by crawling. And before they can stand firmly on their feet, they will often fall. This is what the natural learning process looks like for every human being. So go ahead, it's best to practice speaking from the very beginning of your learning, and then speak, speak, and speak again as much as possible. And if you would like to quickly memorize the most useful German phrases in a practical way, as I described in this episode, learn to speak fluently and understand what Germans say at a glance , join my express German course in six months. The link to the course can be found under this recording. And we'll see each other next Sunday. Czeus. Yeah.

in this video I will outline a plan to
learn German to a basic level of proficiency for beginning language
Learners if you are interested in how to best learn a language and want more
details consider buying my book which you can find in the description this
plan will take 250 to 350 hours of study depending on your focus and dedication
and it will cost you about $150 the first and most important step
is to gather your materials the most important book in
this plan is asmil German with ease for which you must also buy the audio
recordings this course is rather expensive but it contains hands down the
highest quality audio recordings for beginners German the next three books
the berlit self teer the Cortina method and German made simple are all older
methods and can be purchased for a few dollars each through AB ebooks.com or
Amazon all of these courses have two things in common first they contain a
substantial amount of German text and second they contain a complete English
translation parallel to the German text in General whenever you are picking
materials for beginning a language you want to make sure that each course has
either a complete facing translation or a complete answer key you gain nothing
from trying to guess at what a text means on your own and you substantially
slow down your progress if you ever have a doubt about the meaning of anything
you want to be able to find the answer immediately and not fumble around in
dictionaries and grammar books this is simply the most efficient way to learn
on your own once you have gathered your
materials the next step is to make a plan for how and when you will use them
as said follow following this plan you can arrive at a decent level of German
after 250 to 350 hours of study that works out to almost a year if you study
1 hour every day but it can be done much more quickly if you spend 2 3 4 or even
more hours each day studying I'm going to assume that you will be studying 1
hour per day and that it will take you about 250 hours but you need to be
flexible and constantly adjust your plan as you track your progress the
overarching structure of the plan is as follows for the first 100 days we will
be using the asml German with ease course at a rate of introducing one new
lesson per day from Days 101 to 120 you'll be using the Cortina method at a
rate of one new lesson per day from Days 121 to 135 you'll be working through the
berlit self teacher from Days 136 to 150 you'll be working through German made
simple and from Days 151 to 250 you're going to repeat and review
everything now that you have the overarching plan in place you must know
how to spend each hour of your studying time the goal of every hour you spend
studying is to expose yourself to As Much German as possible how well you
know a language correlates directly with how many sentences you have heard read
and understood so focus on maximizing the time that you spend reading
listening to and repeating the text the first 100 days are devoted to finishing
the asml course you should add one new lesson every day the first 30 minutes of
your study session should be devoted to review of the previous three lessons so
if lesson 11 is your new lesson for the day day you would start by reviewing
lesson 8 Once then reviewing lesson 9 twice and then lesson 10 three times
then you will spend 25 minutes studying lesson 11 your goal should be able to
read and listen to the text as many times as possible in those 25 minutes
you should end your hour by previewing the lesson for the following day every
seventh day you should not add a new lesson but rather review the previous
six lessons as many times as you can in an hour be sure to be flexible and
honest with yourself this may take long than 100 days if you are really not
understanding your most recent lessons you may have to take a day or even a
week off to review the entire course up until that point and then start moving
forward again you do not need to know everything perfectly but after the third
day of reviewing your lesson you should be able to read it from start to finish
without any help and also listen to it and understand it without looking at the
text if you can't do this stop and slow down and review until you can and then
move forward after you've gone through the Asal course in this way you should
start feeling more confident in your ability to understand German and your
brain and ear should have begun to grow accustomed to the sounds and rhythms of
German the next step is to spend about 50 days going through the other three
courses all of these have bilingual texts so you should not have too much of
a problem understanding them especially in the beginning lessons your goal with
each of these courses is to be able to take a lesson and read it from beginning
to end without having to look at the translation just as with the asml course
you should spend nearly half of your study session each day reviewing
previous lessons and the second half reading and repeating the new material
again if you ever feel stuck take a few days in which you do not add any new
material but rather review the previous lessons once you have made it through
all of these three courses which should take 50 to 100 hours the Final Phase
involves repeating and reviewing everything you have done so far so as to
consolidate your knowledge during this phase you should spend 45 minutes of
each hour repeatedly listening to the audio from the Asimo course while
following along in the text the remaining 15 minutes should be devoted
to gradually reworking through all of the three other
courses also during this last phase your German will be good enough to profit
from listening to the asmil dialogues outside of your hour of study as well so
let your daily lessons plan repeat while you are driving walking cleaning or

information and The rhythms of a language on a deeper level also feel

but make sure that this does not interfere with your main study

A question I get asked all the time is
milk. What's the best way of memorizing vocabulary? In this video, I'm going to
give you these five easy tips of how you can best memorize the vocabulary. By the
way, if you're new here, my name is Milk and I really enjoy learning German.
Finding yourself in a situation where you have to learn so many words at the
same time, especially if you have like prepare for an exam or something like
that, it can actually be very challenging. I remember myself when I
started learning German. I had a time pressure of wanting to finish learning
German pretty quickly and then go on to do my medical licensing exam work in
German. This was pretty intense. I remember opening this book and looking
at it and I'm like what? I have to memorize this whole book. I was like,
man, how do I go about this? I want to just share with some of you guys some of
these methods that I basically came up with that really helped me make my
vocabulary stick. So if you're that person who feels like you actually know
German and then you open your mouth to speak and then at some point you feel
yourself like you're stuck and you cannot just go on because somehow the
word you wanted to use has disappeared from the head and all of a sudden the
head feels blank. I got you. I really understand what it feels like. And so
let me just share these tips with you. The first method I would recommend if
you're learning vocabulary would be actually using flashcards. Flash cards
for me were a game changer. I remember getting introduced to flash cards when I
was in medical school. I mean when I was studying medicine in Uganda. These were
really helpful. Of course, by then I was actually using physical cards. These are
cards I was kind of writing out for myself. They were very helpful in
memorizing anatomy and some of the facts in medicine like SI units and other
things that don't have sense to them other than just simply getting them into
your head. But at some point pen and paper cards they kind of get messy at
some point if you have to write something and then rub and maybe
something's not right or sometimes they actually just get lost all together. I
remember when I got to find out about this app called Anki. Man, Anki was like
a total game changer because the cards right now can even be e-cards where you
don't have to carry physical cards wherever you go every time. You can even
have the card simply on your phone and you simply go through them. And Anki
does an excellent job at this. Anki uses a method called SRS. It's called space
reputation system. It's like AI power and helps you revise what you actually
need to learn rather than wasting time on what you already know because we have
a tendency as you know students. If you've been a student, you know this
that every time you go to study, you kind of start with the first pages of
what you already know in the first place. So you end up wasting time
learning the things you already know and then leaving out the things you've never
tackled all together. It helps because it kind of forces you to revise more
what you need to revise in other words what you're forgetting and then it keeps
on bringing those cards at those intervals which turns out to be very
very very helpful. Yes, it's a game changer when it comes to vocabulary. So
use this app called Anki. You don't have to use physical cards. Anki actually can
also be downloaded on your laptop. As I talk right now, I have an Anki app on my
laptop. There is also a version on the phone and it's actually free for Android
users. So I mean why not? Now the other technique of effectively learning
vocabulary is using association. The brain learns better with associating
words with images or even situations and stories. And sometimes it even taps into
the feeling of a thing. Memory sticks better that way. What I would recommend
is if you're using flashcards for you to tap into this technique, try to add
images to your flash cards. I'm not saying that you go around taking
thousands of images all over the place and you keep on putting them into Anki
but as long as you can have a picture connotation of the exact word that you
want to represent. For example, if you want to say here is the lamp and here is
called d lamp of do. It helps if you have like a picture of a light bulb
there, you know, which is shining a bit of light. Maybe the lamp represents that
somebody is reading. Then you start to actually associate the lamp with
reading, which is soalled. You understand what I mean? Kind of you're
associating the same setting. And so every time you hear of the word the
lamp, it is providing light and somebody reading. Or for some people who know
this, your word is the lamp unto my feet in that regard. Yes, just kind of like a
torch. So associate imagery to the words. Put images to those cards and
kind of tap into the association effect of how the brain best lands vocabulary.

now you probably already have some goals you're trying to achieve when it comes
to your language learning progress while achieving these goals is important
making sure you make the right goals is the real key to success the very first tip is to set smart goals
smart is an acronym meaning each letter in the word stands for another word the
earliest known reference to smart goals was in an article written by george t
doran for a 1981 issue of the management review academic journal the acronym
varies depending on its use but each letter generally stands for some
criterion that helps with effective goal setting for our purposes let's define
smart goals as follows the s stands for specific
your goals should target a specific area for improvement our natural tendency is
to have a goal that's very general if your goal isn't specific enough you'll
lack the focus and proper direction you need to achieve your goals so s is for
specific m stands for measurable your goals
should be quantifiable they should be able to indicate progress in some way
you have to be able to track your progress otherwise you won't know if
you're getting any closer to your goal as you see yourself getting closer and
closer to your goal your motivation will go up so your goals need to be
measurable a stands for achievable your goals have
to be achievable many people want to become fluent in
their target language immediately however this goal is unrealistic your
goals have to be achievable if your goal is too challenging for your
current level it will only demotivate you when you aren't where you think you
should be instead think about what results can
realistically be achieved given your level your resources and any constraints
such as time so make sure that your goal is actually achievable r stands for relevant
your goals may be specific they may be measurable and they may be achievable
but are they actually relevant to what you want to achieve don't just do a lot
of things if you're focused on improving your
speaking skills in your target language make sure that you spend your time
having conversations with others make sure you're doing the right things
so that your efforts actually bring you closer to your goal instead of just
giving you more work [Music] t stands for timely you need to set a deadline for your goals if you don't
specify when you plan to achieve the result you've set for yourself it's very
easy to put off the task you can delay it until tomorrow the next week or the
next month and at this rate you'll never get things done so your goal must have
an end date so remember tip number one is to set smart goals specific
measurable achievable relevant and timely
so for example a goal you could make is registering for a challenging test a
test that's a little bit beyond your current level i hate failing so if i
register for a challenging test i'm motivated to study because i don't want
to fail this is a good example of a smart goal
because tests are specific there are tons of different
tests focused on speaking grammar and comprehension pick a test that can
measure the specific area in which you'd like to grow
measurable tests are measurable every test measures your performance to some
degree whether it's a total count of right and
wrong answers or a simple pass or fail every test measures your performance
tests are achievable there's an important detail to remember here though
find a test that is achievable for you if you're a beginner then the most
advanced test is probably not right for you find one that's meant for beginners
then after that one work your way up to more advanced tests in the future
tests are relevant most if not all language tests are designed to ensure
that you're capable of performing to a set standard in your target language
lower level tests are designed to ensure that you can handle the most essential
aspects of your target language but there are tests for all levels including
higher education entry exams that could be difficult even for native speakers
pick the one that's right for you and finally tests are timely if your test is
completed in a physical location then this one is obvious you have to be at
that spot at the set time ready to take the test there's no wiggle room but even
online tests will most likely have a deadline for you to complete them
[Music] the second tip to help you stop
